Browse All / Clear Cell Sarcoma of Kidney

Clear Cell Sarcoma of Kidney

MONDO:0005006 Pathograph 13 Show in embeddings browser kidney sarcoma

Clear cell sarcoma of the kidney (CCSK) is a rare malignant renal sarcoma of early childhood. A 1985 clinicopathologic series concluded that, until it is shown to be related to nephroblastoma (Wilms tumour), identifying it as a non-Wilms sarcoma seems appropriate, and that it appears to be the most common renal sarcoma of childhood. In the US SEER 18 registries (2000 to 2017) its age-adjusted incidence among children and adolescents aged 0 to 19 years was 0.205 per million, reported as stable over the years and third among paediatric renal tumours, with incidence rate ratios of 2.5 for boys and 21.1 for children aged 4 years or younger. In a National Wilms Tumor Study Group Pathology Center review, mean age at diagnosis among the National Wilms Tumor Study trial patients was 36 months (range 2 months to 14 years), and the review reported a male to female ratio of 2:1. Abdominal mass and haematuria were the usual presenting signs in an early series, and the primary tumour is typically large and mucoid, with foci of necrosis and prominent cyst formation. Most tumours carry a somatic internal tandem duplication (ITD) clustering in the C terminus of BCOR, within the sequence encoding the PUFD domain, which is important for forming the variant polycomb repressive complex 1; in one series the ITD was found in all 20 CCSKs and in none of 193 other paediatric renal tumours tested. A minority instead carry a YWHAE-FAM22 fusion transcript from t(10;17)(q22;p13), detected in 6 of 50 further CCSKs in one cohort; the FAM22 members involved include FAM22B and FAM22E, now NUTM2B and NUTM2E. In a cohort of 159 CCSKs the BCOR ITD and the YWHAE fusion were mutually exclusive, and 11.8% of tumours carried neither when tested by those assays. In a 39-patient cohort BCOR ITD, YWHAE rearrangement and BCOR::CCNB3 fusion were mutually exclusive, one tumour carried the BCOR::CCNB3 fusion, and three had none of them detected. The skeleton, almost invariably including the skull, was the commonest metastatic site in the 1985 series. Among 37 relapses after treatment on SIOP and AIEOP protocols, 95% were metastatic and the brain was the commonest relapse site, and a review attributes to improved intensive chemotherapy a change in the clinical course, with late relapses now less frequent and the brain having overtaken bone as the most common site of relapse. Treatment combines nephrectomy, performed first on NWTS-based strategies and after preoperative chemotherapy in most patients on SIOP trials, with chemotherapy and radiotherapy. On the National Wilms Tumor Study-5, the 108 eligible patients, treated with vincristine, doxorubicin and cyclophosphamide alternating with cyclophosphamide and etoposide for 24 weeks (regimen I) with radiation to sites of disease, had five-year event-free and overall survival of 79% and 90%. The Children's Oncology Group study AREN0321 omitted radiotherapy for stage I disease when lymph nodes were sampled, with no local recurrences among the seven stage I patients treated without it, and added carboplatin for stage IV disease. Stage IV patients appeared to benefit from the carboplatin-containing regimen, although their outcomes remained unsatisfactory; only four were treated on AREN0321, and the apparent benefit rests on ten stage IV patients from AREN0321 and the AREN03B2 biology study combined, compared with earlier NWTS-5 results. After relapse in the SIOP and AIEOP series, five-year event-free and overall survival were 18% and 26%.

⚙

Pathophysiology

4
BCOR Internal Tandem Duplication
The recurrent lesion of CCSK: a somatic internal tandem duplication (ITD) clustering in the C terminus of BCOR, within the sequence encoding the PUFD domain, which is important for forming the variant polycomb repressive complex 1 and through which BCOR binds PCGF1 in that complex. BCOR can act as a transcriptional corepressor: it interacts selectively with the POZ domain of BCL-6 and can potentiate BCL-6 repression. Somatic ITDs were found in 23 of 27 paediatric CCSKs (85%) from two independent cohorts, identical in primary and relapsed tumour pairs and absent from adjacent normal kidney and blood, and mutant BCOR transcripts and proteins were markedly upregulated in ITD-positive tumours. In another series the ITD was found in all 20 CCSKs and in none of 193 other paediatric renal tumours, and these CCSKs expressed only an aberrant BCOR allele, which the authors read as a close correlation between the BCOR aberration and CCSK tumorigenesis. Expression profiling of patient tumours suggested that CCSK arises within a renal mesenchymal cell, a later study suggested a primitive mesodermal cell able to differentiate towards both nephrons and stroma, and the cell of origin is not established.

Primary Renal Tumor Growth The duplication is the recurrent lesion of CCSK. Its link to tumour formation rests on its presence in most tumours and on the tumours expressing only an aberrant BCOR allele, which the authors read as a close correlation with tumorigenesis; the intervening steps are not established in the cited sources.

PMID:26573325 SUPPORT DIRECT PRIMARY RESULT Human Clinical
"Here we report on the identification of somatic internal tandem duplications (ITDs) clustering in the C terminus of BCOR in 23 of 27 (85%) pediatric clear cell sarcomas of the kidney (CCSK) from two independent cohorts. We profile CCSK tumours using a combination of whole-exome, transcriptome..."
Roy et al. profiled paediatric clear cell sarcomas of the kidney from two independent cohorts by whole-exome, transcriptome and targeted sequencing. They report somatic BCOR internal tandem duplications (ITDs) clustering in the BCOR C terminus in 23 of 27 (85%) tumours, identical ITDs in primary and relapsed tumour pairs, no ITD in adjacent normal kidney or blood, and markedly upregulated mutant BCOR transcripts and proteins in ITD-positive tumours. The quoted abstract sentences state each part of the claim directly; the abstract does not say how many relapsed pairs or normal samples were tested.
PMID:26098867 SUPPORT DIRECT PRIMARY RESULT Human Clinical
"We identified internal tandem duplications in the BCOR gene (BCL6 corepressor) affecting the C terminus in 100% (20/20) of CCSK tumors but in none (0/193) of the other pediatric renal tumors. CCSK tumors expressed only an aberrant BCOR allele, indicating a close correlation between BCOR..."
The authors report their own finding: in their series, internal tandem duplications in BCOR affecting the C terminus were present in all 20 CCSK tumours and in none of 193 other paediatric renal tumours, and the CCSK tumours expressed only an aberrant BCOR allele. They read this as a close correlation between the BCOR aberration and CCSK tumorigenesis. This supports the node as a recurrent lesion of CCSK which, in this series, was absent from all the other paediatric renal tumours tested. The 100% figure comes from a single 20-tumour series and is not a frequency for CCSK as a whole. The abstract does not say whether the duplications are somatic, and it reports a correlation, not a demonstrated causal mechanism.
PMID:27000436 SUPPORT DIRECT BACKGROUND Human Clinical
"Internal tandem duplication within the BCOR gene sequence that encodes the PUFD domain, important in the formation of the non-canonical or variant polycomb repressor complex 1 (v-PRC1), was very recently described in 100% of 20 clear cell sarcomas of kidney (CCSKs)."
Kenny et al. open the abstract of their clear cell sarcoma of kidney cohort study by restating earlier work: the BCOR internal tandem duplication lies within the gene sequence that encodes the PUFD domain, and that domain is important in forming the non-canonical (variant) polycomb repressor complex 1. The sentence states both points outright. Binding the node's protein complex to GO:0140261 BCOR complex is the curator's mapping of the sentence's variant polycomb repressor complex 1 onto the GO class whose definition names the corepressor BCOR among its subunits; it is not a claim the sentence makes. Kenny et al. restate both points rather than test them, and this item does not rely on the sentence's figure for the earlier series. The sentence does not say that BCOR acts as a transcriptional corepressor, and it does not say whether the duplication raises or lowers the activity of the complex.

YWHAE::NUTM2 Fusion
The alternative recurrent lesion: t(10;17)(q22;p13), with breakpoints mapped to 17p13.3 and 10q22.3 in an index tumour, rearranges YWHAE and FAM22 into an in-frame YWHAE-FAM22 fusion transcript joining exons 1-5 of YWHAE to exons 2-7 of FAM22; the FAM22 members involved include FAM22B and FAM22E, now NUTM2B and NUTM2E. The transcript was detected in 6 of 50 further CCSKs tested (12% in that cohort). In a cohort of 159 CCSKs the BCOR internal tandem duplication and the YWHAE fusion were mutually exclusive, and 11.8% of tumours carried neither when tested by those assays. In a 39-patient cohort tested by PCR, FISH or Sanger sequencing, a YWHAE rearrangement (1 tumour), BCOR ITD (34) and BCOR::CCNB3 fusion (1) were mutually exclusive, and 3 tumours had none of them detected.

NUTM2E hgnc:23448 HUGO Gene Nomenclature Committee (hgnc) Relation: this pathophysiological event involves this gene This pathophysiological event involves NUTM2E (hgnc:23448). hgnc:23448 is a gene from the HUGO Gene Nomenclature Committee.

Primary Renal Tumor Growth The fusion occurs in a minority of tumours in place of the BCOR duplication; the steps between it and tumour growth are not characterised in the cited sources.

PMID:22294382 SUPPORT DIRECT PRIMARY RESULT Human Clinical
"Using fluorescently labelled bacterial artificial chromosomes (BACs) and a chromosome-walking strategy on an index case of CCSK with t(10;17)(q22;p13) by karyotype, we identified the chromosomal breakpoints on 17p13.3 and 10q22.3. The translocation results in rearrangement of YWHAE on chromosome..."
O'Meara et al. used fluorescently labelled BACs and chromosome walking on an index CCSK with t(10;17)(q22;p13) to map the breakpoints to 17p13.3 and 10q22.3. The translocation rearranges YWHAE on chromosome 17 and FAM22 on chromosome 10 into an in-frame YWHAE-FAM22 fusion transcript, joining exons 1 to 5 of YWHAE to exons 2 to 7 of FAM22 (structure determined by RT-PCR), and the transcript was detected in six of 50 further CCSKs tested, which the authors report as an overall incidence of 12% in their cohort. The FAM22 members involved in these cases include FAM22B and FAM22E, which HGNC records as previous symbols of NUTM2B (hgnc:23445, NUT family member 2B) and NUTM2E (hgnc:23448, NUT family member 2E). YWHAE-FAM22 is therefore the fusion this node calls YWHAE::NUTM2.
PMID:27000436 SUPPORT DIRECT PRIMARY RESULT Human Clinical
"We analysed a cohort of 159 CCSKs and can now not only confirm that there is indeed mutual exclusivity of these BCOR and YWHAE mutations, but also show that a substantial proportion (in this series 11.8%) of CCSKs bear neither mutation when tested by these assays, raising the possibility of..."
Kenny et al. tested 159 clear cell sarcomas of the kidney for the two lesions their abstract has just named, the BCOR internal tandem duplication and the YWHAE-NUTM2 transcript, and confirm that the two are mutually exclusive. In this series 11.8% of tumours carried neither when tested by these assays, which the authors read as raising the possibility of distinct aetiologies for subsets of CCSK. This is the largest cohort cited here for the exclusivity, and because the abstract names the fusion as YWHAE-NUTM2, placing its lesion under this node needs no inference. The abstract does not give the number of tumours carrying each lesion, and 'neither' is relative to the assays used, as the sentence itself says.
PMID:36563883 SUPPORT INDIRECT PRIMARY RESULT Human Clinical
"BCOR ITD, YWHAE rearrangement, BCOR-CCNB3 fusion and TP53 status were examined by polymerase chain reaction, fluorescence in situ hybridization, or Sanger sequencing in a cohort of 39 patients with CCSK. Among them, 34 cases (87.18%) had BCOR ITD, 1 (2.56%) had YWHAE rearrangement, and 1 (2.56%)..."
In a cohort of 39 CCSK patients tested by PCR, FISH or Sanger sequencing, 34 tumours (87.18%) carried a BCOR internal tandem duplication, one (2.56%) a YWHAE rearrangement and one (2.56%) a BCOR-CCNB3 fusion. No tumour carried more than one of these lesions, and 3 (7.69%) had none of them detected. The mutual exclusivity of the YWHAE and BCOR-CCNB3 lesions rests on a single tumour each. The abstract calls the YWHAE lesion a rearrangement and does not name its partner gene, so reading it as the YWHAE::NUTM2 fusion is an inference.
Primary Renal Tumor Growth
Growth of the primary tumour in the kidney. Typical gross features in a National Wilms Tumor Study Group Pathology Center series were large size (mean diameter 11.3 cm), a mucoid texture, foci of necrosis and prominent cyst formation.

Distant Metastasis to Bone, Brain and Lung
Show evidence (1 reference)
PMID:10632483 SUPPORT DIRECT PRIMARY RESULT Human Clinical
"Typical gross features included large size (mean diameter 11.3 cm), a mucoid texture, foci of necrosis, and prominent cyst formation."
In the authors' own review of clear cell sarcoma of the kidney cases at the NWTSG Pathology Center, the typical gross features of the tumour were large size (mean diameter 11.3 cm), a mucoid texture, foci of necrosis and prominent cyst formation. Large size supports the node's account of the primary renal tumour growing into a large mass; the other three features describe its gross appearance. The abstract does not say how many of the reviewed cases the mean diameter was calculated over, so the figure belongs to this series and not to the disease as a whole.
Distant Metastasis to Bone, Brain and Lung
Spread of the tumour to distant organs. In a 1985 series of 21 children the skeleton was the commonest metastatic site (12 patients), with the skull almost invariably involved (10 children). Among 37 relapses in a combined SIOP and AIEOP series, 35 (95%) were metastatic, and the commonest relapse sites were the brain (13), lungs (7) and bone (5), each counted as a relapse confined to that site.

Show evidence (2 references)
PMID:4065884 SUPPORT DIRECT PRIMARY RESULT Human Clinical
"The most common site for metastasis was the skeleton (12 patients), and the skull was almost invariably involved (10 children)."
Primary result from the authors' clinicopathologic series of 21 children with clear cell sarcoma of the kidney, reported in 1985: the skeleton was the most frequent metastatic site (12 patients), and the skull was almost invariably involved (10 children). The series size comes from the abstract's first sentence, not from this one. These counts describe one early cohort and are not a current rate for the disease. Under this node the item supports the bone component only; the quoted sentence does not mention brain or lung metastases.
PMID:24937667 SUPPORT DIRECT PRIMARY RESULT Human Clinical
"Thirt-five out of thirty-seven relapses (95%) were metastatic; the most common sites of relapse were the brain (n=13), lungs (n=7) and bone (n=5)."
Of the 37 relapses in the SIOP and AIEOP cohort, 35 (95%) were metastatic, and the brain (13), lungs (7) and bone (5) were the commonest relapse sites, so relapsed CCSK is almost always distant spread, most often to these three organs. The full text counts these as relapses confined to that site, so they are not totals of all brain, lung or bone involvement, and the ranking describes relapse rather than metastasis at diagnosis. 'Thirt-five' is the source's own spelling and is kept as written.
✶

Histopathology

4
Classic Pattern of Cords and Fibrovascular Septa
The classic histological pattern of CCSK is cords or nests of tumour cells separated by fibrovascular septa. The cells are described as epithelioid, or as round or spindle-shaped with clear cytoplasm, with round to ovoid nuclei and inconspicuous nucleoli. The septa are thin and regularly branching, ranging from a thin chicken-wire arrangement to broad sheets containing an arborizing capillary vasculature, and myxoid pools also separate the cords. The classic pattern is found at least focally in the majority of tumours.
Show evidence (2 references)
PMID:30736802 SUPPORT DIRECT BACKGROUND Human Clinical
"On histological examination, classic CCSK is characterized by nests or cords of epithelioid cells with round to oval nuclei. Myxoid pools and thin and regularly branching fibrovascular septa separate the cords of tumor cells"
In the introduction to their cyclin D1 study, Uddin et al. describe classic CCSK as nests or cords of epithelioid cells with round to oval nuclei, separated by myxoid pools and by thin, regularly branching fibrovascular septa. The sentences restate the published description, for which the authors cite earlier papers, rather than report the study's own cases.
PMID:24488803 SUPPORT DIRECT BACKGROUND Human Clinical
"The classic microscopic pattern of CCSK includes cords of round or spindle-shaped cells with clear cytoplasm and ovoid to rounded vesicular nuclei with inconspicuous nucleoli. The cells are surrounded by fibrovascular septa ranging from a thin "chicken-wire" like arrangement to broad sheets..."
The introduction of Karlsson et al.'s expression-profiling study describes the classic pattern as cords of round or spindle-shaped cells with clear cytoplasm and ovoid to rounded vesicular nuclei, surrounded by fibrovascular septa that range from a thin chicken-wire arrangement to broad sheets containing an arborizing capillary vasculature, and states that the pattern is found at least focally in the majority of tumours. It cites Argani et al. (2000), the first author and year of the National Wilms Tumor Study Group Pathology Center review cited elsewhere in this entry (PMID:10632483), whose abstract names the classic pattern without describing it. The sentences restate that description rather than report the study's own tumours, and they give no proportion behind the majority.
Variant Histologic Patterns
CCSK shows several histological patterns besides the classic one. A National Wilms Tumor Study Group Pathology Center review identified nine major patterns (classic, myxoid, sclerosing, cellular, epithelioid, palisading, spindle, storiform and anaplastic), and virtually all tumours contained multiple patterns that blended with one another. Citing that review, a later paper lists the components mixed with the classic pattern in descending order of frequency as myxoid, sclerosing, cellular, epithelioid, palisading, spindle-cell, storiform and anaplastic. A review notes that this diversity of patterns can mimic most other paediatric renal tumours and often leads to confusion and misdiagnosis.
Show evidence (3 references)
PMID:10632483 SUPPORT DIRECT PRIMARY RESULT Human Clinical
"Nine major histologic patterns were identified (classic, myxoid, sclerosing, cellular, epithelioid, palisading, spindle, storiform, and anaplastic); virtually all tumors contained multiple patterns that blended with one another."
In their own review of clear cell sarcoma of the kidney cases at the National Wilms Tumor Study Group Pathology Center, Argani et al. identified nine major histological patterns and found that virtually all tumours contained multiple patterns that blended with one another. The abstract lists the patterns without describing them and gives no frequency for any single pattern.
PMID:24488803 SUPPORT DIRECT BACKGROUND Human Clinical
"This classic pattern is found at least focally in the majority of the tumors, but is often mixed with other histological components such as (in descending order of frequency) myxoid, sclerosing, cellular, epithelioid, palisading, spindle-cell, storiform, and anaplastic elements (Argani et al., 2000)."
Citing Argani et al. (2000), the introduction of this expression-profiling study states that the classic pattern is often mixed with other histological components and lists them in descending order of frequency, from myxoid to anaplastic. It restates the earlier review and gives no frequency for any component.
PMID:30917048 SUPPORT DIRECT REVIEW SYNTHESIS Human Clinical
"Histologically, this tumor exhibits a great diversity of morphologic patterns that can mimic most other pediatric renal neoplasms, often leading to confusion and misdiagnosis."
This review states that CCSK shows a great diversity of morphological patterns that can mimic most other paediatric renal tumours, often leading to confusion and misdiagnosis. It is the review's synthesis of published series and gives no rate of misdiagnosis.
Vimentin-Positive, Cytokeratin-Negative and MIC2-Negative Immunophenotype
In the National Wilms Tumor Study Group Pathology Center review, immunohistochemical stains were performed on 45 cases and only vimentin was consistently immunoreactive. All CCSKs were cytokeratin-negative, including epithelioid tumours that mimicked Wilms tumour, and MIC2-negative, including cellular tumours that mimicked primitive neuroectodermal tumour, and these consistently negative results helped exclude other tumours in the differential diagnosis.
Show evidence (1 reference)
PMID:10632483 SUPPORT DIRECT PRIMARY RESULT Human Clinical
"Immunohistochemical stains were performed on 45 cases; only vimentin was consistently immunoreactive. Consistently negative results with other antibodies helped exclude other tumors in the differential diagnosis; all CCSKs were cytokeratin-negative, including epithelioid tumors that mimicked..."
Argani et al. stained 45 cases of their review by immunohistochemistry. Only vimentin was consistently immunoreactive, and all CCSKs were negative for cytokeratin, including epithelioid tumours that mimicked Wilms tumour, and for MIC2, including cellular tumours that mimicked primitive neuroectodermal tumour. The authors report that these consistently negative results helped exclude other tumours in the differential diagnosis. The abstract names no other antibodies and does not say how many of the 45 tumours were stained with each.
BCOR Immunoreactivity
A review states that immunohistochemistry for BCOR has been shown to be a sensitive and specific marker for CCSK in the context of paediatric renal tumours. In one series, strong diffuse nuclear BCOR immunoreactivity was seen in 15 of 18 CCSKs (83%) and in 2 of 10 Wilms tumours, and none of the malignant rhabdoid tumours or congenital mesoblastic nephromas showed any immunoreactivity, so the lack of strong diffuse staining does not exclude CCSK. Strong BCOR staining is not confined to CCSK: five renal or perirenal sarcomas in patients aged 30 to 62 years, which raised the differential diagnosis of CCSK because of their morphology and strong BCOR immunoreactivity, proved on RNA sequencing to be malignant solitary fibrous tumours.
Show evidence (3 references)
PMID:30917048 SUPPORT DIRECT REVIEW SYNTHESIS Human Clinical
"Immunohistochemistry for BCOR has also been shown to be a sensitive and specific marker for clear cell sarcoma of the kidney in the context of pediatric renal tumors."
This review states that BCOR immunohistochemistry has been shown to be a sensitive and specific marker for CCSK among paediatric renal tumours. It is the review's synthesis of published studies, its scope is paediatric renal tumours, and the abstract gives no sensitivity or specificity figures.
PMID:34673711 SUPPORT DIRECT PRIMARY RESULT Human Clinical
"Strong, diffuse nuclear immunoreactivity for cyclin D1 and BCOR was noted in 61% (n=11/18) and 83% (n=15/18) of CCSK, respectively, while it was significantly less in WT (n=3/10 for cyclin D1) (n=2/10 for BCOR). None of the MRT and CMN examples demonstrated any immunoreactivity."
Singh et al. stained CCSKs and Wilms tumours on tissue microarrays, and the malignant rhabdoid tumours and congenital mesoblastic nephromas individually. Strong, diffuse nuclear BCOR staining was seen in 15 of 18 CCSKs (83%) and in 2 of 10 Wilms tumours, which the authors report as significantly less, and none of the rhabdoid tumours or mesoblastic nephromas showed any immunoreactivity. Strong diffuse staining was therefore not seen in every CCSK. The series is small, and the cyclin D1 results in the same sentence are covered under the Wilms Tumor differential diagnosis.
PMID:30864973 SUPPORT DIRECT PRIMARY RESULT Human Clinical
"We report 5 such undifferentiated renal/perirenal sarcomas which raised the differential diagnosis of CCSK due to their morphologic appearance and strong BCOR immunoreactivity, but which on RNA sequencing proved to be malignant solitary fibrous tumors (SFTs). The neoplasms occurred in patients..."
In a 2019 series, Argani et al. report five undifferentiated renal or perirenal sarcomas whose morphology and strong BCOR immunoreactivity raised the differential diagnosis of CCSK, and which RNA sequencing showed to be malignant solitary fibrous tumours. The patients were aged 30 to 62 years, so the series does not bear on the review's statement about paediatric renal tumours, but it shows that strong BCOR staining also occurs in a tumour that mimics CCSK.

PMID:30917048 SUPPORT DIRECT REVIEW SYNTHESIS Human Clinical
"Clear cell sarcoma of the kidney is an uncommon malignant pediatric renal neoplasm that typically presents in the 2- to 3-year age group and has a propensity for aggressive behavior and late relapses."
This review opens by stating that clear cell sarcoma of the kidney is an uncommon malignant paediatric renal neoplasm, which is the finding this phenotype records. The statement is the review's synthesis of published series.

BCOR
Gene: BCOR hgnc:20893 gene page HUGO Gene Nomenclature Committee (hgnc) Relation: this disease-associated gene is this gene This disease-associated gene is BCOR (hgnc:20893). hgnc:20893 is a gene from the HUGO Gene Nomenclature Committee. relationship_type: SOMATIC_DRIVER variant_origin: SOMATIC

PMID:26573325 SUPPORT DIRECT PRIMARY RESULT Human Clinical
"Here we report on the identification of somatic internal tandem duplications (ITDs) clustering in the C terminus of BCOR in 23 of 27 (85%) pediatric clear cell sarcomas of the kidney (CCSK) from two independent cohorts. We profile CCSK tumours using a combination of whole-exome, transcriptome..."
Roy et al. profiled paediatric clear cell sarcomas of the kidney from two independent cohorts by whole-exome, transcriptome and targeted sequencing. They report somatic BCOR internal tandem duplications (ITDs) clustering in the BCOR C terminus in 23 of 27 (85%) tumours, identical ITDs in primary and relapsed tumour pairs, no ITD in adjacent normal kidney or blood, and markedly upregulated mutant BCOR transcripts and proteins in ITD-positive tumours. The quoted abstract sentences state each part of the claim directly; the abstract does not say how many relapsed pairs or normal samples were tested.
PMID:36563883 SUPPORT DIRECT PRIMARY RESULT Human Clinical
"BCOR ITD, YWHAE rearrangement, BCOR-CCNB3 fusion and TP53 status were examined by polymerase chain reaction, fluorescence in situ hybridization, or Sanger sequencing in a cohort of 39 patients with CCSK. Among them, 34 cases (87.18%) had BCOR ITD, 1 (2.56%) had YWHAE rearrangement, and 1 (2.56%)..."
In a cohort of 39 CCSK patients tested by PCR, FISH or Sanger sequencing, 34 tumours (87.18%) carried a BCOR internal tandem duplication, one (2.56%) a YWHAE rearrangement and one (2.56%) a BCOR-CCNB3 fusion. No tumour carried more than one of these lesions, and 3 (7.69%) had none of them detected. The mutual exclusivity of the YWHAE and BCOR-CCNB3 lesions rests on a single tumour each.

Medical Actions

Radical Nephrectomy
Action: radical nephrectomyNCI Thesaurus (NCIT) Relation: this treatment is this clinical intervention This treatment is radical nephrectomy (NCIT:C51778). NCIT:C51778 is a clinical intervention from the NCI Thesaurus. Ontology label: Radical Nephrectomy NCIT:C51778
Platform: Surgery
Surgical removal of the tumour-bearing kidney. In a Shanghai multicentre cohort treated following NWTS strategies, treatment comprised upfront radical nephrectomy followed by radiotherapy and intensive chemotherapy, and the authors note that upfront surgery gives a definite pathological diagnosis. On the SIOP 93-01 and SIOP 2001 trials, 169 of 191 CCSK patients received chemotherapy before nephrectomy and all underwent total nephrectomy.

PMID:38980838 SUPPORT DIRECT PRIMARY RESULT Human Clinical
"Treatment comprised upfront radical nephrectomy, followed by radiotherapy and intensive chemotherapy. ... Upfront surgery of NWTS strategies can make a definite pathology diagnosis, but how to reduce tumor rupture during surgery is important especially in developing countries."
Zhang et al. studied children with CCSK treated following NWTS strategies at children's hospitals in Shanghai. Treatment comprised upfront radical nephrectomy, followed by radiotherapy and intensive chemotherapy. In their conclusions the authors say the upfront surgery of NWTS strategies gives a definite pathological diagnosis, and that reducing tumour rupture during surgery is important, especially in developing countries. The sentences summarise this cohort's NWTS-based protocol, not a cooperative-group protocol, so they support the claim for this cohort rather than for every patient treated on NWTS strategies.
PMID:23880476 SUPPORT DIRECT PRIMARY RESULT Human Clinical
"We analysed the prospectively collected data of patients with a histologically verified CCSK, entered onto SIOP 93-01/2001 trials. ... Pre-operative chemotherapy was administered to 169/191 patients. All patients underwent total nephrectomy and 189/191 patients received post-operative chemotherapy."
The SIOP Renal Tumour Study Group analysed prospectively collected data on CCSK patients entered onto the SIOP 93-01 and SIOP 2001 trials. Pre-operative chemotherapy was given to 169 of 191 patients, all underwent total nephrectomy, and 189 of 191 received post-operative chemotherapy, so on these SIOP protocols most CCSK patients had chemotherapy before surgery. The report says nothing about NWTS, and it calls the operation total nephrectomy rather than radical nephrectomy, so it supports the timing contrast but not the specific radical procedure.
Regimen I Chemotherapy
Action: chemotherapyNCI Thesaurus (NCIT) Relation: this treatment is this clinical intervention This treatment is chemotherapy (NCIT:C15632). NCIT:C15632 is a clinical intervention from the NCI Thesaurus. Ontology label: Chemotherapy NCIT:C15632
Agent: vincristine CHEBI:28445 Chemical Entities of Biological Interest (CHEBI) Relation: this treatment uses this therapeutic agent This treatment uses vincristine (CHEBI:28445). CHEBI:28445 is a therapeutic agent from Chemical Entities of Biological Interest. doxorubicin CHEBI:28748 Chemical Entities of Biological Interest (CHEBI) Relation: this treatment uses this therapeutic agent This treatment uses doxorubicin (CHEBI:28748). CHEBI:28748 is a therapeutic agent from Chemical Entities of Biological Interest. cyclophosphamide CHEBI:4027 Chemical Entities of Biological Interest (CHEBI) Relation: this treatment uses this therapeutic agent This treatment uses cyclophosphamide (CHEBI:4027). CHEBI:4027 is a therapeutic agent from Chemical Entities of Biological Interest. etoposide CHEBI:4911 Chemical Entities of Biological Interest (CHEBI) Relation: this treatment uses this therapeutic agent This treatment uses etoposide (CHEBI:4911). CHEBI:4911 is a therapeutic agent from Chemical Entities of Biological Interest.
Platform: Small molecule
On the single-arm National Wilms Tumor Study-5, the 108 eligible patients received vincristine/doxorubicin/cyclophosphamide alternating with cyclophosphamide/etoposide for 24 weeks (regimen I) with radiation to sites of disease, and had five-year event-free survival of 79% and overall survival of 90%. Five-year event-free survival for stages I to IV was 100%, 88%, 73% and 29%, from groups of 12, 44, 45 and 7 patients, so the stage I and stage IV figures rest on small numbers. The outcome was similar to the earlier NWTS-4 regimen but reached over a shorter treatment duration, and the authors concluded that regimen I showed more benefit for stage I and II disease than for higher stages, where new therapies are needed.
Effect differs by subgroup:
baseline severity stages I and II vs higher stage disease (stages III and IV) — larger effect on disease control compared with the three-drug NWTS-4 regimen cross study comparison
Comparison of the single-arm NWTS-5 trial with the earlier NWTS-4 trial, with NWTS-4 patients reclassified by NWTS-5 staging criteria. The paper reports no formal test of a stage-by-treatment interaction, so the stage gradient is a cross-trial observation rather than a tested subgroup effect. For higher-stage disease the authors found no apparent benefit of regimen I over the NWTS-4 regimen.

PMID:30255545 SUPPORT DIRECT PRIMARY RESULT Human Clinical
"NWTS-4 patients were reclassified using NWTS-5 staging criteria. ... The NWTS-5 results suggest that patients with stages I and II CCSK treated with regimen I have better disease control than NWTS-4 patients who were treated with three drugs delivered over 15 months. For higher stage disease,..."
The NWTS-5 report states the stage-dependent effect: patients with stage I and II disease treated with regimen I had better disease control than NWTS-4 patients treated with three drugs over 15 months, while for higher-stage disease the benefit of regimen I was not apparent. The comparison is with a separate, earlier trial whose patients were reclassified by NWTS-5 staging criteria, not a randomised comparison.

PMID:30255545 SUPPORT DIRECT PRIMARY RESULT Human Clinical
"Treatment consisted of vincristine/doxorubicin/cyclophosphamide alternating with cyclophosphamide/etoposide for 24 weeks and radiation to sites of disease. ... One hundred eight eligible patients were enrolled on study (69% males, 63% Caucasian), with a median age of 22 months. Stage..."
Abstract of the NWTS-5 report on its own prospective single-arm trial in clear cell sarcoma of the kidney. Treatment was vincristine/doxorubicin/cyclophosphamide alternating with cyclophosphamide/etoposide for 24 weeks, with radiation to sites of disease; the full-text introduction calls this four-drug chemotherapy regimen I. Of the 108 eligible patients, 12, 44, 45 and 7 had stage I to IV disease. Five-year EFS was 79% and OS 90%, and five-year EFS for stages I to IV was 100%, 88%, 73% and 29%, so the stage I and stage IV figures come from groups of 12 and 7 patients. The authors judge the overall outcome similar to NWTS-4 but reached over a shorter treatment duration, note that brain metastases were more frequent than on NWTS-4, and conclude that regimen I showed more benefit for stage I and II disease than for higher stages, where new therapies are needed. That benefit comes from comparison with the earlier NWTS-4 trial, not from a randomised comparison; the full text attributes it to the chemotherapy because patients on both trials received tumour-bed radiation, and finds no benefit at higher stages. Every NWTS-5 patient also received radiation, so the survival figures belong to the combined-modality protocol, not to regimen I alone.
Carboplatin-Containing Chemotherapy for Stage IV Disease
Action: chemotherapyNCI Thesaurus (NCIT) Relation: this treatment is this clinical intervention This treatment is chemotherapy (NCIT:C15632). NCIT:C15632 is a clinical intervention from the NCI Thesaurus. Ontology label: Chemotherapy NCIT:C15632
Agent: carboplatin CHEBI:31355 Chemical Entities of Biological Interest (CHEBI) Relation: this treatment uses this therapeutic agent This treatment uses carboplatin (CHEBI:31355). CHEBI:31355 is a therapeutic agent from Chemical Entities of Biological Interest.
Platform: Small molecule
The Children's Oncology Group study AREN0321 added carboplatin for stage IV disease (regimen UH-1). Only four stage IV patients were treated on AREN0321, which the authors judge too few to determine whether regimen UH-1 improved outcomes compared with NWTS-5. Pooled with patients enrolled only on the AREN03B2 biology study who received the same stage-appropriate chemotherapy, the ten stage IV patients had a four-year event-free survival of 58.3%, against a five-year event-free survival of 29% on NWTS-5, and on this basis stage IV patients appeared to benefit from the carboplatin-containing regimen, although their outcomes remained unsatisfactory. The comparison is with earlier NWTS-5 results, not a concurrent control arm.

PMID:38396300 SUPPORT DIRECT PRIMARY RESULT Human Clinical
"In the Children's Oncology Group study AREN0321 of risk-adapted therapy, RT was omitted for stage I disease if lymph nodes were sampled, and carboplatin was added for stage IV disease (regimen UH-1). ... Patients with stage IV disease appeared to benefit from a carboplatin-containing regimen,..."
The COG report on AREN0321 states the study's change for stage IV disease, the addition of carboplatin (regimen UH-1), alongside its omission of radiotherapy for stage I disease when lymph nodes were sampled. It concludes that stage IV patients appeared to benefit from the carboplatin-containing regimen, although their outcomes remained unsatisfactory. That conclusion pools AREN0321 patients with patients enrolled only on AREN03B2 who received the same stage-appropriate chemotherapy, and compares them with earlier NWTS-5 results rather than a concurrent control arm. The benefit is credited to the intensified regimen as a whole, not to carboplatin alone, and the authors note that the stage IV group is small.
PMID:38396300 SUPPORT DIRECT PRIMARY RESULT Human Clinical
"When combining studies, the 4-year EFS rates for patients who had stage I (n = 10), II (n = 47), III (n = 65), and IV (n = 10) disease were 90% (95% CI, 73.2%-100.0%), 93.4% (95% CI, 86.4%-100.0%), 82.8% (95% CI, 74.1%-92.6%), and 58.3% (95% CI, 34%-100.0%), respectively. ... Patients with..."
The results give ten stage IV patients across the pooled AREN0321 and AREN03B2 cohorts, with a four-year EFS of 58.3% (95% CI 34% to 100%). The discussion states that only four stage IV patients were treated on AREN0321, too few to determine whether regimen UH-1 improved outcomes compared with NWTS-5, and that the pooled outcome suggests chemotherapy intensification may be beneficial when set against the five-year EFS of 29% on NWTS-5. The apparent benefit therefore rests on ten patients and on a comparison of a four-year pooled figure with a five-year historical one.
Radiation Therapy
Action: radiation therapyNCI Thesaurus (NCIT) Relation: this treatment is this clinical intervention This treatment is radiation therapy (NCIT:C15313). NCIT:C15313 is a clinical intervention from the NCI Thesaurus. Ontology label: Radiation Therapy NCIT:C15313
Platform: Radiotherapy
Radiotherapy to sites of disease accompanied regimen I on the National Wilms Tumor Study-5, where every patient received postoperative radiation to the tumour bed (10 Gy) and to other infradiaphragmatic and metastatic sites as necessary, whatever the completeness of resection. The Children's Oncology Group study AREN0321 omitted radiotherapy for stage I disease when lymph nodes were sampled; none of the seven stage I patients treated without it had a local recurrence, and one stage I recurrence occurred in the brain. The authors relate their better stage I outcomes, compared with SIOP's, to staging: with nephrectomy first, COG staging selects a very small and highly favourable stage I group for omission of radiotherapy, 7.6% of their patients against 39.5% stage I in the SIOP studies. The single-arm NWTS-5 trial cannot separate the contribution of radiotherapy from that of the chemotherapy.

PMID:30255545 SUPPORT INDIRECT PRIMARY RESULT Human Clinical
"Treatment consisted of vincristine/doxorubicin/cyclophosphamide alternating with cyclophosphamide/etoposide for 24 weeks and radiation to sites of disease. ... Five-year EFS and OS were 79% (95% CI: 71%-88%) and 90% (95% CI: 84%-96%). Five-year EFS for stage I-IV was 100%, 88%, 73%, and 29%,..."
From the NWTS-5 report on its own prospective single-arm trial. The abstract gives the protocol, vincristine/doxorubicin/cyclophosphamide alternating with cyclophosphamide/etoposide for 24 weeks with radiation to sites of disease, and its outcome: five-year EFS 79%, OS 90%, and five-year EFS for stages I to IV of 100%, 88%, 73% and 29%. The full-text methods and discussion describe the radiation: postoperative radiation to the tumour bed (10 Gy) and to other infradiaphragmatic and metastatic sites as necessary, given to all sites of disease however completely the tumour was resected. This directly establishes radiotherapy as part of first-line CCSK therapy on NWTS-5, but supports its effect on outcome only indirectly, because every patient also received the chemotherapy and there was no unirradiated group. The authors attribute the stage I and II improvement over NWTS-4 to the chemotherapy, because patients on both trials received tumour-bed radiation, and state that the role of radiation therapy in this cancer needs to be further elucidated.
PMID:38396300 SUPPORT DIRECT PRIMARY RESULT Human Clinical
"In the Children's Oncology Group study AREN0321 of risk-adapted therapy, RT was omitted for stage I disease if lymph nodes were sampled, and carboplatin was added for stage IV disease (regimen UH-1). ... There were no local recurrences among seven patients with stage I disease who were treated..."
The COG report on AREN0321 states that radiotherapy was omitted for stage I disease when lymph nodes were sampled. None of the seven stage I patients treated without radiotherapy had a local recurrence. The one stage I recurrence was in the brain, a distant site that the authors judge local radiotherapy would likely not have prevented, so the result shows no local failure after leaving out radiotherapy, not an absence of relapse. The authors note that the stage I group is small.
PMID:38396300 SUPPORT DIRECT PRIMARY RESULT Human Clinical
"The seemingly superior outcomes for patients with stage I CCSK treated on the COG regimens suggests that stage I CCSK based on upfront nephrectomy is more favorable than stage I CCSK after neoadjuvant chemotherapy. This observation makes intuitive sense given that staging differences between COG..."
The COG authors compare their stage I results with SIOP's and suggest that stage I CCSK staged on upfront nephrectomy is more favourable than stage I CCSK staged after neoadjuvant chemotherapy. COG staging selected a very small and highly favourable subgroup for omission of radiotherapy: 10 of 132 patients (7.6%) were stage I, against 75 of 191 (39.5%) in the SIOP studies. The absence of local recurrence after omitting radiotherapy therefore comes from a small, favourable group selected by COG staging.
Salvage Chemotherapy for Relapsed Disease
Action: salvage chemotherapyNCI Thesaurus (NCIT) Relation: this treatment is this clinical intervention This treatment is salvage chemotherapy, annotated with Chemotherapy (NCIT:C15632). NCIT:C15632 is a clinical intervention from the NCI Thesaurus. Ontology label: Chemotherapy NCIT:C15632 Regimen: ICE regimenNCI Thesaurus (NCIT) Relation: this regimen component is this clinical intervention This regimen component is ICE regimen (NCIT:C10345). NCIT:C10345 is a clinical intervention from the NCI Thesaurus. Ontology label: ICE Regimen NCIT:C10345
Agent: ifosfamide CHEBI:5864 Chemical Entities of Biological Interest (CHEBI) Relation: this treatment uses this therapeutic agent This treatment uses ifosfamide (CHEBI:5864). CHEBI:5864 is a therapeutic agent from Chemical Entities of Biological Interest. carboplatin CHEBI:31355 Chemical Entities of Biological Interest (CHEBI) Relation: this treatment uses this therapeutic agent This treatment uses carboplatin (CHEBI:31355). CHEBI:31355 is a therapeutic agent from Chemical Entities of Biological Interest. etoposide CHEBI:4911 Chemical Entities of Biological Interest (CHEBI) Relation: this treatment uses this therapeutic agent This treatment uses etoposide (CHEBI:4911). CHEBI:4911 is a therapeutic agent from Chemical Entities of Biological Interest.
Platform: Small molecule
Chemotherapy for relapse after complete remission. In a combined SIOP and AIEOP series, 30 of 37 relapsed patients received chemotherapy in different combinations, 19 had surgical excision and 18 radiotherapy, and 14 went on to high-dose chemotherapy with autologous bone marrow transplantation; 22 of the 37 reached a second complete remission, 15 of whom relapsed again. Five of the 13 patients with an isolated brain relapse received ICE (ifosfamide, carboplatin and etoposide), and three of them achieved a long-term complete response, which the authors read as possibly promising for brain relapse while noting that they have no data on the influence of upfront treatment; one patient died of septic shock after one cycle of ICE. The agents and regimen bound here are those of ICE, one of the combinations used. No standard treatment guideline was available for relapsed CCSK except in the United Kingdom, and the authors state that too few relapsed patients have been reported to establish the contributions of chemotherapy, high-dose chemotherapy with stem-cell transplantation, radiotherapy and surgery, so no standard treatment guidelines are available for this patient group.
Mechanism Target:
INHIBITS Distant Metastasis to Bone, Brain and Lung — Relapse in the series was metastatic in 35 of 37 patients, so chemotherapy at relapse is given against distant metastatic disease. The second complete remissions followed relapse treatment as a whole, which combined chemotherapy with surgery and radiotherapy, so they cannot be credited to chemotherapy alone.

High-Dose Chemotherapy with Autologous Stem-Cell Rescue
Action: high-dose chemotherapy with autologous bone marrow transplantationNCI Thesaurus (NCIT) Relation: this treatment is this clinical intervention This treatment is high-dose chemotherapy with autologous bone marrow transplantation, annotated with Autologous Hematopoietic Stem Cell Transplantation (NCIT:C16039). NCIT:C16039 is a clinical intervention from the NCI Thesaurus. Ontology label: Autologous Hematopoietic Stem Cell Transplantation NCIT:C16039
Platform: Cell therapy
High-dose chemotherapy followed by autologous bone marrow transplantation (ABMT) for relapsed disease. In the combined SIOP and AIEOP series it was given to 14 of 37 relapsed patients after chemotherapy, surgery and/or radiotherapy. Among the patients who completed the intended relapse treatment it was given to 13 of the 21 who reached a second complete remission and to 1 of the 7 who did not, a comparison the authors say their numbers do not allow them to test statistically. They conclude that intensive treatment aiming for local control, followed by high-dose chemotherapy and ABMT, seems to be of benefit to enhance survival. Summarising their series and earlier reports, 12 of 24 relapsed patients so treated were alive without disease after a median follow-up of 52 months, but the authors caution that the treatment is mostly given to patients who have already reached a second complete remission and that it carries direct and late toxicity. No standard treatment guidelines are available for relapsed CCSK, because too few relapsed patients have been reported to establish the contribution of each treatment.
Mechanism Target:
INHIBITS Distant Metastasis to Bone, Brain and Lung — Relapse in the series was metastatic in 35 of 37 patients, and high-dose chemotherapy with ABMT followed the treatment of those relapses in 14 patients.

Comparing the patients who completed their intended relapse treatment, the authors found high-dose chemotherapy in 13 of the 21 who reached a second complete remission and in 1 of the 7 who did not. They state that the series is too small for statistical analysis, so the contrast is descriptive, and patients who reach remission are more likely to be offered consolidation.
PMID:24937667 SUPPORT DIRECT REVIEW SYNTHESIS Human Clinical
"Summarising our study and previous reports, in total 24 relapsed CCSK patients received high-dose chemotherapy followed by ABMT, of which 12 patients (50%) were alive without disease after a median follow-up of 52 months (range, 9–103 months) ... This seems to be promising, although it should be..."
Pooling their own series with earlier reports, the authors count 24 relapsed CCSK patients treated with high-dose chemotherapy and ABMT, 12 of whom (50%) were alive without disease after a median follow-up of 52 months. They call this promising but stress that the treatment is mostly given to a selected group already in second complete remission, and that its direct and late toxicity must be weighed against the risk of death from the disease. The pooled figure combines this series with work the paper did not perform, and it carries the selection the authors describe.

Progression

2
Presentation in early childhood
Age: 2 months to 14 years (mean 36 months)
Mean age at diagnosis was 36 months (range 2 months to 14 years) among the National Wilms Tumor Study trial patients of a National Wilms Tumor Study Group Pathology Center review, and a review describes typical presentation in the 2- to 3-year age group.
Show evidence (2 references)

"Mean age at diagnosis in the NWTS group was 36 months with a range of 2 months to 14 years. The male to female ratio was 2:1."
This National Wilms Tumor Study Group Pathology Center case series of clear cell sarcoma of the kidney reports a mean age at diagnosis of 36 months, with a range of 2 months to 14 years, in its NWTS group (the cases entered on National Wilms Tumor Study Group trials), which gives the age range of this phase directly. PMID:30917048 SUPPORT DIRECT REVIEW SYNTHESIS Human Clinical
"Clear cell sarcoma of the kidney is an uncommon malignant pediatric renal neoplasm that typically presents in the 2- to 3-year age group and has a propensity for aggressive behavior and late relapses."
This review states that clear cell sarcoma of the kidney typically presents in the 2- to 3-year age group, which places the usual presentation in early childhood. The review summarises published patient series rather than reporting its own cohort.
Relapse
In a combined SIOP and AIEOP series, 37 of 237 patients (16%) relapsed after complete remission, at a median of 17 months from diagnosis; 35 of the 37 relapses (95%) were metastatic, most often to the brain (13), lungs (7) and bone (5), and five-year event-free and overall survival after relapse were 18% and 26%. A review describes a propensity for late relapse and attributes to improved intensive chemotherapy regimens a change in the clinical course, with late relapses now less frequent and the brain having overtaken bone as the most common site of relapse.
Show evidence (2 references)
PMID:24937667 SUPPORT DIRECT PRIMARY RESULT Human Clinical
"Thirty-seven of 237 CCSK patients (16%) treated according to SIOP and AIEOP protocols developed a relapse. Median time from initial diagnosis to relapse was 17 months (range, 5.5 months - 6.6 years). Thirt-five out of thirty-seven relapses (95%) were metastatic; the most common sites of relapse..."
Among 237 CCSK patients treated on SIOP and AIEOP protocols, 37 (16%) relapsed after complete remission at the end of primary treatment, at a median of 17 months from diagnosis. 35 of the 37 relapses (95%) were metastatic, most often to the brain (13), lungs (7) and bone (5). Five-year event-free and overall survival after relapse, both measured from the date of relapse, were 18% (95% CI 4% to 32%) and 26% (95% CI 10% to 42%), so outcome after relapse is poor. 'Thirt-five' is the source's own spelling and is kept as written.
PMID:30917048 SUPPORT DIRECT REVIEW SYNTHESIS Human Clinical
"Clear cell sarcoma of the kidney is an uncommon malignant pediatric renal neoplasm that typically presents in the 2- to 3-year age group and has a propensity for aggressive behavior and late relapses. ... Improved intensive chemotherapy regimens have influenced the clinical course of the..."
This review of clear cell sarcoma of the kidney states that the tumour typically presents in the 2- to 3-year age group and has a propensity for aggressive behaviour and late relapses. It then attributes a change in the clinical course to improved intensive chemotherapy regimens, with late relapses now less frequent and the brain having overtaken bone as the most common site of relapse. Both statements are the review's synthesis of published clinical series, and the review gives no time threshold for a late relapse.
🪜

Stages

4
Stage I
On the National Wilms Tumor Study-5 (NWTS-5), 12 of the 108 staged patients had stage I disease, and five-year event-free survival for stage I was 100%. On the Children's Oncology Group (COG) studies AREN0321 and AREN03B2, 10 of 132 patients (7.6%) were stage I, with a pooled four-year event-free survival of 90%. AREN0321 omitted radiotherapy for stage I disease when lymph nodes were sampled; none of the seven stage I patients treated without it had a local recurrence, and one stage I recurrence occurred in the brain. COG stage I rests on upfront nephrectomy and SIOP stage I on the tumour after preoperative chemotherapy, so a tumour that would be COG stage II or III if resected upfront could be SIOP stage I. The COG authors cite 39.5% of CCSK patients as stage I in the SIOP studies against 7.6% under COG staging, and suggest that stage I based on upfront nephrectomy is more favourable than stage I after neoadjuvant chemotherapy. On the SIOP 93-01 and SIOP 2001 trials, 2 of 80 stage I patients received radiotherapy.
The SIOP 93-01 and SIOP 2001 report gives 42% of its 191 patients as stage I, and its radiotherapy figures count 80 stage I patients, whereas the COG report gives 39.5% (75 of 191) for the same studies. Neither cited text explains the difference.
Show evidence (5 references)
PMID:30255545 SUPPORT DIRECT PRIMARY RESULT Human Clinical
"Stage distribution was as follows: stage I, 12; II, 44; III, 45; IV, 7. ... Five-year EFS for stage I-IV was 100%, 88%, 73%, and 29%, respectively."
The NWTS-5 abstract gives the stage distribution of the trial's 108 eligible patients (12, 44, 45 and 7 for stages I to IV) and a five-year event-free survival of 100% for stage I. The figure rests on 12 patients, all of whom also received radiation to the tumour bed on this protocol.
PMID:38396300 SUPPORT DIRECT PRIMARY RESULT Human Clinical
"When combining studies, the 4-year EFS rates for patients who had stage I (n = 10), II (n = 47), III (n = 65), and IV (n = 10) disease were 90% (95% CI, 73.2%-100.0%), 93.4% (95% CI, 86.4%-100.0%), 82.8% (95% CI, 74.1%-92.6%), and 58.3% (95% CI, 34%-100.0%), respectively."
Pooling AREN0321 with the AREN03B2 patients who received AREN0321 stage-appropriate chemotherapy, the COG report gives a four-year event-free survival of 90% (95% CI 73.2% to 100%) for the ten stage I patients. The figure is four-year where the NWTS-5 one is five-year, and it rests on ten patients.
PMID:38396300 SUPPORT DIRECT PRIMARY RESULT Human Clinical
"In the Children's Oncology Group study AREN0321 of risk-adapted therapy, RT was omitted for stage I disease if lymph nodes were sampled, and carboplatin was added for stage IV disease (regimen UH-1). ... There were no local recurrences among seven patients with stage I disease who were treated..."
The COG report states the stage I change on AREN0321: radiotherapy was omitted for stage I disease when lymph nodes were sampled. None of the seven stage I patients treated without radiotherapy had a local recurrence, and the one stage I recurrence was in the brain, a distant site. The result shows no local failure after radiotherapy was left out in a small group, not an absence of relapse.
+ 2 more references
Stage II
On NWTS-5, 44 of the 108 staged patients had stage II disease, with five-year event-free and overall survival of 88% and 98%. NWTS-5 classified any invasion of the renal sinus vessels as stage II, where NWTS-4 had classified invasion within the hilar plane as stage I in the absence of other stage II disease, and NWTS-4 patients were reclassified by the NWTS-5 criteria for comparison. On AREN0321 and AREN03B2, the 47 stage II patients had a pooled four-year event-free survival of 93.4%. Stage II and III patients on AREN0321 received regimen I with radiotherapy; there were no local recurrences among the 47 stage II patients, and the authors suggest that omitting radiotherapy for stage II disease could be investigated in a future study.
Show evidence (5 references)

The NWTS-5 abstract gives 44 stage II patients among the trial's 108 eligible patients (12, 44, 45 and 7 for stages I to IV) and a five-year event-free survival of 88% for stage II.
PMID:30255545 SUPPORT DIRECT Human Clinical
"Patients on NWTS-4 with invasion of the renal sinus vessels within the hilar plane (a line joining the most medial aspects of the upper and lower poles) were classified as Stage I (if there was no other evidence of Stage II disease). On NWTS-5, patients with any invasion of the renal sinus..."
The methods of the NWTS-5 report state how the trial changed the stage II definition: any invasion of the renal sinus vessels was stage II on NWTS-5, whereas NWTS-4 had classified invasion within the hilar plane as stage I when there was no other evidence of stage II disease. NWTS-4 patients were restaged by the NWTS-5 definition before the two trials were compared. The sentences describe the trial's staging procedure and give one criterion of the stage, not a full definition.
PMID:30255545 SUPPORT DIRECT PRIMARY RESULT Human Clinical
"prognostic factors were assessed by focusing only on the remaining 89 patients with stage II or III disease. The five-year EFS and OS for stage II patients were 88% (95% CI: 77%-99%) and 98% (95% CI: 92%-100%) and for stage III were 73% (95% CI: 57%-87%) and 89% (95% CI: 79%-99%). Prognostic..."
The NWTS-5 results give five-year event-free and overall survival of 88% (95% CI 77% to 99%) and 98% (95% CI 92% to 100%) for stage II. The prognostic factor analysis in the same passage pooled the 89 stage II and III patients, so it does not single out stage II.
+ 2 more references
Stage III
On NWTS-5, 45 of the 108 staged patients had stage III disease, with five-year event-free and overall survival of 73% and 89%. NWTS-5 assessed prognostic factors, tumour spill among them, in its stage II and III patients together: no factor predicted overall survival on multivariate analysis, and only age of 12 months or more correlated with better event-free and overall survival on univariate analysis. On AREN0321 and AREN03B2, the 65 stage III patients had a pooled four-year event-free survival of 82.8%. Stage II and III patients on AREN0321 received regimen I with radiotherapy, all three local recurrences occurred in local stage III disease, and among local stage III tumours neither the initial procedure type, margin status nor lymph node involvement was prognostic. In a multicentre cohort of 54 children treated in Shanghai following NWTS strategies and staged by the NWTS system, five-year event-free survival was 71.1% for the 21 stage III patients. Across that cohort, inferior vena cava tumour thrombus (6 patients; event-free survival 41.7% against 87.4% without it) and tumour rupture before or during surgery (5 patients; 40% against 86.8%) were adverse factors on univariate analysis, and tumour rupture was the only independent adverse factor on multivariate analysis (HR 5.9, 95% CI 1.4 to 24.5). The authors attribute part of their stage III result to tumour rupture, with event-free survival of about 86% for stage III patients without it.

+ 4 more references
Stage IV
Stage IV patients in the cited series had distant metastases at diagnosis. On NWTS-5, 7 of the 108 staged patients had stage IV disease; the lungs were the commonest metastatic site (3 patients), followed by bone (2), five of the seven relapsed, and five-year event-free survival for stage IV was 29%. On AREN0321 and AREN03B2, 7.6% of the 132 patients analysed had metastases (bone 20%, liver 20%, lung only 30%, lung and bone together 30%). AREN0321 added carboplatin for stage IV disease (regimen UH-1). The ten stage IV patients of the two studies combined had a four-year event-free survival of 58.3%, and the authors judge that they appeared to benefit from the carboplatin-containing regimen, although their outcomes remained unsatisfactory; the comparison is with the earlier NWTS-5 result rather than a concurrent control. On the SIOP 93-01 and SIOP 2001 trials, stage IV disease was a significant adverse prognostic factor for event-free survival.

Prevalence

1
United States children and adolescents aged 0-19 years (SEER 18 registries, 2000-2017)
Annual Incidence 0.0205 per 100,000 per year <1 in 1,000,000 per year
Age-adjusted incidence of 0.205 per million, which is 0.0205 per 100,000. The authors report it as stable over the years and third among paediatric renal tumours. The paper gives the rate per million without stating a time unit, so the POPULATION_PER_YEAR denominator follows the SEER convention for incidence rates rather than the quoted text. The authors note that they could not validate diagnoses in the SEER database, so selection bias may affect the estimate.

Population-based SEER registry analysis of US children and adolescents. PMID:10632483 SUPPORT DIRECT PRIMARY RESULT Human Clinical
"Mean age at diagnosis in the NWTS group was 36 months with a range of 2 months to 14 years. The male to female ratio was 2:1."
This National Wilms Tumor Study Group Pathology Center case series of clear cell sarcoma of the kidney reports a mean age at diagnosis of 36 months, with a range of 2 months to 14 years, in its NWTS group (the cases entered on National Wilms Tumor Study Group trials), which gives the mean and range directly. It then reports a male to female ratio of 2:1 in a sentence that names no cohort, so the ratio is attributed to the series rather than to the NWTS group alone. The 36-month mean places the typical age at diagnosis in early childhood, but the abstract gives no breakdown by age band and the range reaches 14 years. The ratio comes from a pathology-center and trial series, not a population-based incidence study.
PMID:34095035 SUPPORT DIRECT PRIMARY RESULT Human Clinical
"Here we further confirm boy and young age are two important risk factors for developing CCSK. The incidence rate ratios of boys and children ≤ 4 years are 2.5 and 21.1, respectively."
The discussion of this SEER registry analysis gives its incidence rate ratios to one decimal place: 2.5 for boys against girls, and 21.1 for children aged 4 years or younger against older children and adolescents. The authors call male sex and young age risk factors for CCSK, meaning associations with higher incidence rather than demonstrated causes.

Differential Diagnoses

3

Conditions with similar clinical presentations that must be differentiated from Clear Cell Sarcoma of Kidney:

Wilms Tumor MONDO:0006058
Overlapping Features A 1985 series concluded that, until CCSK is shown to be related to nephroblastoma, identifying it as a non-Wilms sarcoma seems appropriate. BCOR internal tandem duplication and cyclin D1 immunostaining help distinguish CCSK from blastemal Wilms tumour, but neither test is absolute. In a cohort of 159 CCSKs, 11.8% carried neither the ITD nor the YWHAE fusion, so a negative ITD test does not exclude CCSK. Strong diffuse cyclin D1 staining was seen in only 11 of 18 CCSKs in one tissue-microarray series, and in another the epithelial component of most Wilms tumours stained diffusely and often strongly while the blastema was negative in most, so the cyclin D1 contrast holds for Wilms tumour blastema.
Distinguishing Features
  • BCOR internal tandem duplication, found in most but not all CCSKs, and in none of 193 other paediatric renal tumours tested in one series and none of 18 Wilms tumours in another
  • Diffuse strong cyclin D1 immunostaining in most CCSKs (all 14 cases in one series, 7 of 7 assessable cases in a second, 11 of 18 in a third), against only rare positive nuclei in the blastema of most Wilms tumours in the first series and negative blastema in 18 Wilms tumours in the second; in a fourth series the epithelial component of most Wilms tumours stained, so the contrast applies to blastema
  • Cytokeratin-negative immunostaining in all CCSKs of one series, including epithelioid tumours that mimicked Wilms tumour, where consistently negative stains helped exclude other tumours in the differential diagnosis
Show evidence (9 references)
PMID:26573325 SUPPORT DIRECT PRIMARY RESULT Human Clinical
"BCOR ITDs were not found in a cohort of other childhood renal tumours (18 Wilms tumours and 9 congenital mesoblastic nephromas) and soft-tissue sarcomas (n=10)."
In the study that found BCOR internal tandem duplications in most clear cell sarcomas of the kidney, the duplications were not found in a cohort of other childhood renal tumours that included 18 Wilms tumours and 9 congenital mesoblastic nephromas, nor in 10 soft-tissue sarcomas. This directly supports BCOR ITD as a feature that separates CCSK from Wilms tumour.
PMID:26098867 SUPPORT INDIRECT PRIMARY RESULT Human Clinical
"We identified internal tandem duplications in the BCOR gene (BCL6 corepressor) affecting the C terminus in 100% (20/20) of CCSK tumors but in none (0/193) of the other pediatric renal tumors."
In this series, BCOR internal tandem duplications were found in all 20 CCSK tumours and in none of the 193 other paediatric renal tumours tested, so the lesion separated CCSK from every other paediatric renal tumour examined. The abstract does not say which tumour types made up the 193 or how many were Wilms tumours, so its bearing on the Wilms tumour differential rests on the premise that Wilms tumours were among them.
PMID:4065884 SUPPORT INDIRECT PRIMARY RESULT Human Clinical
"Until substantial proof is found that clear cell sarcoma of the kidney is related to nephroblastoma, its identification as a non-Wilms' sarcoma would seem to be appropriate."
The authors conclude that, until clear cell sarcoma of the kidney is shown to be related to nephroblastoma, identifying it as a non-Wilms sarcoma seems appropriate. This supports treating Wilms tumour as a separate disease from CCSK. That the two must be told apart in diagnosis follows only by inference, from the relation between them that the sentence leaves open. The sentence describes no clinical, imaging or histological feature that distinguishes the two tumours, so it supports no distinguishing feature in this row.
+ 6 more references
Neuroblastoma MONDO:0005072
Overlapping Features Neuroblastoma is one of the paediatric tumours against which CCSK markers have been tested. Cyclin D1 immunostaining, which helps separate CCSK from Wilms and rhabdoid tumours, does not separate it from neuroblastoma, which also stained in both series (all four cases diffusely in one, 18 of 29 in the other).
Show evidence (2 references)
PMID:25556515 SUPPORT DIRECT PRIMARY RESULT Human Clinical
"Cyclin D1 expression was evaluated in 59 renal tumours-CCSK (14), WT (25), rhabdoid tumour (four), Ewing sarcoma (five), and CMN (11)-and four neuroblastomas. All 14 CCSKs showed diffuse and strong reactivity. In contrast, the blastematous component of most WTs showed only rare positive nuclei,..."
Mirkovic et al. stained the following for cyclin D1: 14 CCSKs, 25 Wilms tumours and four rhabdoid tumours (all among 59 renal tumours that also included Ewing sarcomas and CMNs), plus four neuroblastomas. Every CCSK stained diffusely and strongly. The blastema of most Wilms tumours showed only rare positive nuclei, and the rhabdoid tumours showed rare to focal staining, whereas all four neuroblastomas stained diffusely (moderate to strong). The authors conclude that cyclin D1 helps distinguish CCSK from Wilms tumour and rhabdoid tumour but not from neuroblastoma. The rhabdoid and neuroblastoma comparisons rest on four tumours each.
PMID:25751590 SUPPORT DIRECT PRIMARY RESULT Human Clinical
"Excluding a case with poor tissue section antigenicity, 7 of 7 CCSKs (100%) showed diffuse and strong nuclear cyclin D1 staining. Cyclin D1 immunohistochemistry was also performed on tissue microarrays of other pediatric renal tumors: blastemal areas of 18 WT cases were negative; 6 rhabdoid..."
A second series, by a different group, shows the same direction for Wilms tumour, rhabdoid tumour and neuroblastoma. The two series differ on mesoblastic nephroma: this one reports positive staining in 3 mesoblastic nephromas and concludes that cyclin D1 does not separate them from CCSK, whereas the first found it helpful for some. Cyclin D1 was diffuse and strong in 7 of 7 assessable CCSKs (one case was excluded for poor antigenicity). On tissue microarrays of other paediatric renal tumours, the blastemal areas of 18 Wilms tumours were negative and 6 rhabdoid tumours showed patchy and weak staining, while 18 of 29 neuroblastomas stained. The authors conclude that cyclin D1 helps distinguish CCSK from blastemal Wilms tumour and rhabdoid tumours but not from neuroblastomas. Neuroblastoma staining was frequent but not universal in this series, and the comparator tumours were stained on tissue microarray cores rather than whole sections.
Rhabdoid Tumor MONDO:0002729
Overlapping Features Renal rhabdoid tumour (the RTK subtype of the Rhabdoid Tumor entry) is a paediatric renal tumour that cyclin D1 immunostaining helps separate from CCSK.
Distinguishing Features
  • Rare to focal cyclin D1 immunoreactivity in renal rhabdoid tumours in one series, and patchy, weak staining in another, against diffuse strong staining in CCSK

name: Clear Cell Sarcoma of Kidney
creation_date: '2026-10-28T18:40:00Z'
description: >-
  Clear cell sarcoma of the kidney (CCSK) is a rare malignant renal sarcoma of
  early childhood. A 1985 clinicopathologic series concluded that, until it is
  shown to be related to nephroblastoma (Wilms tumour), identifying it as a
  non-Wilms sarcoma seems appropriate, and that it appears to be the most
  common renal sarcoma of childhood. In the US SEER 18 registries (2000 to
  2017) its age-adjusted incidence among children and adolescents aged 0 to 19
  years was 0.205 per million, reported as stable over the years and third
  among paediatric renal tumours, with incidence rate ratios of 2.5 for boys
  and 21.1 for children aged 4 years or younger. In a National Wilms Tumor
  Study Group Pathology Center review, mean age at diagnosis among the National
  Wilms Tumor Study trial patients was 36 months (range 2 months to 14 years),
  and the review reported a male to female ratio of 2:1. Abdominal mass and
  haematuria were the usual presenting signs in an early series, and the
  primary tumour is typically large and mucoid, with foci of necrosis and
  prominent cyst formation. Most tumours carry a somatic internal tandem
  duplication (ITD) clustering in the C terminus of BCOR, within the sequence
  encoding the PUFD domain, which is important for forming the variant
  polycomb repressive complex 1; in one series the ITD was found in all 20
  CCSKs and in none of 193 other paediatric renal tumours tested. A minority
  instead carry a YWHAE-FAM22 fusion transcript from t(10;17)(q22;p13),
  detected in 6 of 50 further CCSKs in one cohort; the FAM22 members involved
  include FAM22B and FAM22E, now NUTM2B and NUTM2E. In a cohort of 159 CCSKs
  the BCOR ITD and the YWHAE fusion were mutually exclusive, and 11.8% of
  tumours carried neither when tested by those assays. In a 39-patient cohort
  BCOR ITD, YWHAE rearrangement and BCOR::CCNB3 fusion were mutually
  exclusive, one tumour carried the BCOR::CCNB3 fusion, and three had none of
  them detected. The skeleton, almost invariably including the skull, was the
  commonest metastatic site in the 1985 series. Among 37 relapses after
  treatment on SIOP and AIEOP protocols, 95% were metastatic and the brain was
  the commonest relapse site, and a review attributes to improved intensive
  chemotherapy a change in the clinical course, with late relapses now less
  frequent and the brain having overtaken bone as the most common site of
  relapse. Treatment combines nephrectomy, performed first on NWTS-based
  strategies and after preoperative chemotherapy in most patients on SIOP
  trials, with chemotherapy and radiotherapy. On the National Wilms Tumor
  Study-5, the 108 eligible patients, treated with vincristine, doxorubicin and
  cyclophosphamide alternating with cyclophosphamide and etoposide for 24
  weeks (regimen I) with radiation to sites of disease, had five-year
  event-free and overall survival of 79% and 90%. The Children's Oncology Group
  study AREN0321 omitted radiotherapy for stage I disease when lymph nodes
  were sampled, with no local recurrences among the seven stage I patients
  treated without it, and added carboplatin for stage IV disease. Stage IV
  patients appeared to benefit from the carboplatin-containing regimen,
  although their outcomes remained unsatisfactory; only four were treated on
  AREN0321, and the apparent benefit rests on ten stage IV patients from
  AREN0321 and the AREN03B2 biology study combined, compared with earlier
  NWTS-5 results. After relapse in the SIOP and AIEOP series,
  five-year event-free and overall survival were 18% and 26%.
categories:
- Pediatric Cancer
- Kidney Cancer
- Sarcoma
parents:
- kidney sarcoma
synonyms:
- CCSK
- clear cell sarcoma of the kidney
- renal clear cell sarcoma
- childhood clear cell sarcoma of the kidney
notes: >-
  MONDO places this term under both kidney sarcoma (MONDO:0002930) and clear
  cell sarcoma (MONDO:0002926), the term bound by Clear_Cell_Sarcoma.yaml. This
  entry covers only the renal tumour of early childhood, whose recurrent
  lesions are BCOR internal tandem duplication and YWHAE::NUTM2 fusion. None of
  the sources cited here discusses clear cell sarcoma of soft tissue, so the
  entry makes no claim about how the two tumours are related.
disease_term:
  preferred_term: clear cell sarcoma of kidney
  term:
    id: MONDO:0005006
    label: clear cell sarcoma of kidney
epidemiology:
- name: Age at diagnosis
  description: >-
    Mean age at diagnosis was 36 months (range 2 months to 14 years) among the
    National Wilms Tumor Study trial patients of a National Wilms Tumor Study
    Group Pathology Center review. In SEER, the incidence rate ratio for
    children aged 4 years or younger was 21.1.
  mean_range: 36 months
  evidence:
  - reference: PMID:10632483
    reference_title: "Clear cell sarcoma of the kidney: a review of 351 cases from the National Wilms Tumor Study Group Pathology Center."
    supports: SUPPORT
    directness: DIRECT
    quote_role: PRIMARY_RESULT
    evidence_source: HUMAN_CLINICAL
    snippet: "Mean age at diagnosis in the NWTS group was 36 months with a range of 2 months to 14 years. The male to female ratio was 2:1."
    explanation: >-
      This National Wilms Tumor Study Group Pathology Center case series of
      clear cell sarcoma of the kidney reports a mean age at diagnosis of 36
      months, with a range of 2 months to 14 years, in its NWTS group (the cases
      entered on National Wilms Tumor Study Group trials), which gives the mean
      and range directly. It then reports a male to female ratio of 2:1 in a
      sentence that names no cohort, so the ratio is attributed to the series
      rather than to the NWTS group alone. The 36-month mean places the typical
      age at diagnosis in early childhood, but the abstract gives no breakdown
      by age band and the range reaches 14 years. The ratio comes from a
      pathology-center and trial series, not a population-based incidence study.
  - reference: PMID:34095035
    reference_title: "Childhood Clear Cell Sarcoma of Kidney: Incidence and Survival."
    supports: SUPPORT
    directness: DIRECT
    quote_role: PRIMARY_RESULT
    evidence_source: HUMAN_CLINICAL
    snippet: "Here we further confirm boy and young age are two important risk factors for developing CCSK. The incidence rate ratios of boys and children ≤ 4 years are 2.5 and 21.1, respectively."
    explanation: >-
      The discussion of this SEER registry analysis gives its incidence rate
      ratios to one decimal place: 2.5 for boys against girls, and 21.1 for
      children aged 4 years or younger against older children and adolescents.
      The authors call male sex and young age risk factors for CCSK, meaning
      associations with higher incidence rather than demonstrated causes.

prevalence:
- population: "United States children and adolescents aged 0-19 years (SEER 18 registries, 2000-2017)"
  measure_type: ANNUAL_INCIDENCE
  prevalence_class: BELOW_1_IN_1000000
  rate_per_100000: 0.0205
  rate_denominator: POPULATION_PER_YEAR
  notes: >-
    Age-adjusted incidence of 0.205 per million, which is 0.0205 per 100,000.
    The authors report it as stable over the years and third among paediatric
    renal tumours. The paper gives the rate per million without stating a time
    unit, so the POPULATION_PER_YEAR denominator follows the SEER convention for
    incidence rates rather than the quoted text. The authors note that they
    could not validate diagnoses in the SEER database, so selection bias may
    affect the estimate.

    explanation: >-
      Population-based SEER registry analysis of US children and adolescents.
      The abstract reports the present age-adjusted incidence of CCSK as 0.205
      per million, describes it as stable over the years, and ranks CCSK third
      among paediatric renal tumours. Stability is the authors' reading of an
      incidence trend plot, and the paper gives the rate per million without a
      time unit.

    explanation: >-
