  therapeutic_modality: SMALL_MOLECULE
  target_mechanisms:
  - target: Distant Metastasis to Bone, Brain and Lung
    treatment_effect: INHIBITS
    description: >-
      Relapse in the series was metastatic in 35 of 37 patients, so
      chemotherapy at relapse is given against distant metastatic disease. The
      second complete remissions followed relapse treatment as a whole, which
      combined chemotherapy with surgery and radiotherapy, so they cannot be
      credited to chemotherapy alone.
    evidence:
    - reference: PMID:24937667
      reference_title: "Treatment and outcome of patients with relapsed clear cell sarcoma of the kidney: a combined SIOP and AIEOP study."
      supports: SUPPORT
      directness: INDIRECT
      quote_role: PRIMARY_RESULT
      evidence_source: HUMAN_CLINICAL
      snippet: "Thirt-five out of thirty-seven relapses (95%) were metastatic; the most common sites of relapse were the brain (n=13), lungs (n=7) and bone (n=5). Relapse treatment consisted of chemotherapy (n=30), surgery (n=19) and/or radiotherapy (n=18), followed by high-dose chemotherapy and autologous bone marrow transplantation (ABMT) in 14 patients. Twenty-two out of thirty-seven patients (59%) achieved a second complete remission (CR); 15 of whom (68%) developed a second relapse."
      explanation: >-
        In the SIOP and AIEOP series, 35 of 37 relapses were metastatic, 30 of
        the 37 patients received chemotherapy as part of relapse treatment,
        and 22 of 37 reached a second complete remission, 15 of whom relapsed
        again. Reading the remissions as an effect of chemotherapy on the
        metastatic disease takes an inference step, because relapse treatment
        also included surgery and radiotherapy and the series was not
        designed to separate them. 'Thirt-five' is the source's own spelling
        and is kept as written.

- name: High-Dose Chemotherapy with Autologous Stem-Cell Rescue
  description: >-
    High-dose chemotherapy followed by autologous bone marrow transplantation
    (ABMT) for relapsed disease. In the combined SIOP and AIEOP series it was
    given to 14 of 37 relapsed patients after chemotherapy, surgery and/or
    radiotherapy. Among the patients who completed the intended relapse
    treatment it was given to 13 of the 21 who reached a second complete
    remission and to 1 of the 7 who did not, a comparison the authors say
    their numbers do not allow them to test statistically. They conclude that
    intensive treatment aiming for local control, followed by high-dose
    chemotherapy and ABMT, seems to be of benefit to enhance survival.
    Summarising their series and earlier reports, 12 of 24 relapsed patients so
    treated were alive without disease after a median follow-up of 52 months,
    but the authors caution that the treatment is mostly given to patients who
    have already reached a second complete remission and that it carries
    direct and late toxicity. No standard treatment guidelines are available
    for relapsed CCSK, because too few relapsed patients have been reported to
    establish the contribution of each treatment.

Create: Clear Cell Sarcoma of Kidney · 2026-10-28T23:24:55Z · View source

New entry for clear cell sarcoma of the kidney (CCSK; MONDO:0005006; NCIT:C4264), a rare paediatric renal sarcoma distinct from Wilms tumour. Part of the neuroblastoma expansion series (issue 15822); claim issue 15848. Content: two epidemiology rows (age at diagnosis, male predominance); a SEER annual-incidence record (children 0-19, 2000-2017); two progression rows (presentation in early childhood; relapse, including the shift from bone to brain and fewer late relapses with modern chemotherapy); four pathophysiology nodes: a somatic BCOR internal tandem duplication (the origin node; renal mesenchymal cell bound to CL:0008019, with the CL searches recorded; molecular function GO:0003714 so the BCOR gene record is grounded), the alternative YWHAE::NUTM2B/E fusion from t(10;17), primary renal tumour growth, and distant metastasis to bone, brain and lung (conforms to invasion_and_metastasis#Metastatic Colonization); four histopathology rows (classic cords and fibrovascular septa, NCIT:C49203; variant patterns, NCIT:C35867; the vimentin-positive, cytokeratin- and MIC2-negative immunophenotype and BCOR immunoreactivity, both NCIT:C40998, with the searches for more specific terms recorded); four phenotypes (abdominal mass, haematuria, bone and brain metastases), all causally connected; a BCOR genetic record with three structured case fractions (85%, 100% and 87.18% in three cohorts); three differentials (Wilms tumour, neuroblastoma, renal rhabdoid tumour, each limited to what cyclin D1, BCOR and cytokeratin studies state, including a series in which the epithelial component of most Wilms tumours was also cyclin D1-positive (PMID:30736802)); and four treatments (radical nephrectomy, NWTS-5 regimen I with CHEBI-bound vincristine, doxorubicin, cyclophosphamide and etoposide and a stage-dependent effect modifier, carboplatin for stage IV on AREN0321, and radiotherapy), with small-number caveats. 66 evidence items from 24 references. Left out on purpose: the planned link to the BCOR sarcoma entry (that PR is on hold); YWHAE, NUTM2B and NUTM2E genetic records (no sourced molecular function for the fusion, so check-gene-activity-grounding would fail without a baseline edit); molecular has_subtypes; TP53 as a cooperating lesion; phenotype frequencies across treatment eras. The notes keep MONDO's placement under clear cell sarcoma and limit the entry to the renal tumour, without claiming how it relates to clear cell sarcoma of soft tissue. The histopathology section was added in a second drafting pass, after a pre-submission review found it missing, and was checked against its sources before commit. Source report: research/Clear_Cell_Sarcoma_of_Kidney-deep-research-claude_code.md (claude_code provider), re-validated before commit, 76 of 76 references resolved. preflight-dr returned WARN: the report's HGNC table is shifted by one row, so no gene identifier was copied from it; every gene was looked up in HGNC. Not committed: 50 uncited caches from the research and design runs. 1. Disease Information
claude-haiku-4-5-20251001, claude-opus-5 36 citations 2026-10-28T19:00:56.766268

1. Disease Information

Overview

Clear cell sarcoma of the kidney (CCSK) is a rare, aggressive malignant mesenchymal (non-epithelial, non-blastemal) renal neoplasm of early childhood. It is conventionally described as the second most common renal malignancy of childhood after Wilms tumour, accounting for roughly 2–5% of paediatric renal tumours (PMID:26573325, PMID:22579455). A SEER analysis ranked it third by incidence among all paediatric renal tumours (PMID:34095035) — both statements appear in the literature and the discrepancy reflects whether benign/borderline entities (congenital mesoblastic nephroma) and renal cell carcinoma are counted.

The tumour was historically known as "bone-metastasising renal tumour of childhood", and was separated from Wilms tumour by Beckwith and Palmer in 1978 on the basis of its distinctive histology and relapse pattern. It is characterised by a unilateral, unicentric mass centred on the renal medulla, a tendency to vascular invasion, and a propensity for late relapse to bone and, in the modern treatment era, especially to brain.

MONDO's own definition (retrieved from OLS4, identical to the NCIT definition plus the BCOR clause):

"A rare pediatric sarcoma affecting the kidney. It is characterized by the presence of epithelioid or spindle cells forming cords or nests, separated by fibrovascular septa. It is associated with internal tandem duplications in the BCOR gene. It metastasizes to lung, bone, brain and soft tissue."

MeSH: There is no dedicated MeSH descriptor for CCSK. It indexes under Kidney Neoplasms (D007680) and Sarcoma, Clear Cell / Sarcoma (D012509). I could not find a CCSK-specific MeSH heading; do not invent one.

Synonyms

CCSK (abbreviation); childhood clear cell sarcoma of the kidney; childhood kidney clear cell sarcoma; childhood renal clear cell sarcoma; clear cell sarcoma of the kidney; kidney clear cell sarcoma; paediatric/pediatric kidney clear cell sarcoma; paediatric/pediatric renal clear cell sarcoma; renal clear cell sarcoma. Historical: bone-metastasising renal tumour of childhood. (MONDO + NCIT synonym sets, retrieved via OLS4.)

Evidence provenance type

Aggregated disease-level, not EHR/individual-patient. The authoritative sources are (i) central-pathology-reviewed cooperative-group cohorts — NWTS 1–5 (PMID:10632483, PMID:14752069, PMID:30255545), COG AREN03B2/AREN0321 (PMID:38396300), SIOP 93-01/2001 (PMID:23880476); (ii) population registries — SEER (PMID:34095035); and (iii) molecular case series. No EHR-derived phenotype profile exists for CCSK, so no kb/phenotype_distributions/ entry should be assembled from this report.

2. Etiology

Disease causal factors

CCSK is a genetically driven somatic neoplasm with a strikingly quiet genome. Three mutually exclusive driver classes account for ~88–100% of cases depending on cohort and assay:

  1. BCOR exon 15 internal tandem duplication (ITD) — the dominant driver.
  2. Ueno-Yokohata et al. (PMID:26098867, Nat Genet 2015), verbatim abstract: "We identified internal tandem duplications in the BCOR gene (BCL6 corepressor) affecting the C terminus in 100% (20/20) of CCSK tumors but in none (0/193) of the other pediatric renal tumors. CCSK tumors expressed only an aberrant BCOR allele, indicating a close correlation between BCOR aberration and CCSK tumorigenesis."
  3. Roy et al. (PMID:26573325, Nat Commun 2015), verbatim: "…somatic internal tandem duplications (ITDs) clustering in the C terminus of BCOR in 23 of 27 (85%) pediatric clear cell sarcomas of the kidney (CCSK) from two independent cohorts."
  4. Astolfi et al. (PMID:26516930, Oncotarget 2015), verbatim: "Three different in-frame ITD in exon15 of BCOR, were detected. The presence of the ITD was confirmed on tumor DNA and cDNA, and resulted in overexpression of BCOR."
  5. Independent confirmation: 10/11 (90.9%) by NGS (PMID:28833375); 34/39 (87.18%) by PCR/FISH/Sanger (PMID:36563883).
  6. YWHAE::NUTM2B/E fusion from t(10;17)(q22;p13) — ~10–12%.
  7. O'Meara et al. (PMID:22294382, J Pathol 2012) mapped the breakpoints to 17p13.3 and 10q22.3 and reported "an in-frame fusion transcript of ∼3 kb, incorporating exons 1-5 of YWHAE and exons 2-7 of FAM22" (FAM22B/FAM22E = NUTM2B/NUTM2E), found in 6/50 (12%) additional CCSKs.
  8. BCOR::CCNB3 fusion — rare. First reported in a kidney presenting as CCSK by Wong et al. (PMID:28833375); further cases reported (e.g. PMID:36533315).

Mutual exclusivity is established. Karlsson et al. (PMID:26493387) concluded that the "YWHAE–NUTM2B/E fusion and the BCOR ITD are mutually exclusive events and activated different downstream signaling systems"; Zhang et al. (PMID:36563883) confirmed "BCOR ITD, YWHAE rearrangement and BCOR-CCNB3 were mutually exclusive" in 39 cases.

A "double-negative" subset persists. Kenny et al. (PMID:27000436, J Pathol 2016) analysed 159 CCSKs, confirmed mutual exclusivity, and found that 11.8% carried neither alteration by their assays, "raising the possibility of distinct aetiologies for subsets of CCSK", with associated clinical differences. Zhang et al. likewise reported 3/39 (7.69%) with none of the three. Some double-negative cases are resolved by newer assays detecting long spliced ITDs (PMID:35836306) or by BCOR::MAML3 (PMID:39871307); others are reclassified on review (e.g. malignant solitary fibrous tumour — see §10).

Secondary/cooperating lesion — TP53. Zhang et al. (PMID:36563883, Hum Pathol 2023), verbatim: "25.64% of the cohort acquired TP53 aberration (10/39, 3 with both copy number deletion and point mutation, 6 with deletion only, and 1 with mutation only), all of which were associated with BCOR ITD… Patients with both BCOR ITD and TP53 deletion had the poorest prognosis." TP53 (hgnc:11998) is therefore a cooperating somatic lesion and candidate prognostic marker, not a second driver class.

Epigenetic co-lesion — TCF21 silencing. See §4 and §6.

Risk factors

Genetic risk factors - Causal somatic variants: as above. These are tumour-restricted. - Germline susceptibility loci: none identified. Gooskens et al. (PMID:26158413) found "No recurrent segmental chromosomal copy number changes or somatic variants (single nucleotide or small insertion/deletion)" beyond the fusion case; Fiore et al. (PMID:36835166) performed WES on nine BCOR-ITD CCSKs and reported "No significant recurrents of somatic or germline mutations other than BCOR-ITD were identified among the evaluated samples", confirming "this tumor carries a low mutational burden". - Modifier genes: none established. - Relationship to germline BCOR disease — important and frequently muddled. Germline loss-of-function BCOR variants cause oculofaciocardiodental (OFCD) syndrome / syndromic microphthalmia 2 (OMIM:300166), X-linked dominant with male lethality. CCSK has not been reported as a feature of OFCD, and the CCSK lesion is a different kind of allele (an in-frame duplication within PUFD, with retained/over-expressed protein) rather than a truncation. Note honestly: a single OFCD patient with T-cell lymphoma has been reported and reviews raise the possibility of a modest cancer risk in OFCD, but there is no evidence of CCSK predisposition, and absence of reports is not the same as demonstrated absence of risk. Do not curate a germline predisposition claim.

Environmental risk factors No established environmental, occupational, dietary, radiation, or infectious risk factor has been identified for CCSK. The only reproducible demographic risk associations are host-constitutional: - Male sex — SEER incidence rate ratio 3 for boys (PMID:34095035); M:F ≈ 2:1 in NWTS (PMID:10632483); 64% male in SIOP (PMID:23880476); 69% male on NWTS-5 (PMID:30255545). - Age under 4 years — SEER incidence rate ratio 21 for children ≤4 years (PMID:34095035). - Family history — no familial clustering reported.

This is a genuine negative and should be curated as such rather than left blank. Any dismech environmental: entry for CCSK should carry the review_notes: waiver sentinel (Left deliberately uncited. followed by ≥20 words naming the searches run), because no quotable exposure evidence exists.

Protective factors

None identified — genetic or environmental. There are no protective alleles, no dietary/lifestyle exposures, and no chemopreventive strategies reported for CCSK. Given its early-childhood onset and somatic-driver biology, primary prevention is not a meaningful concept (see §13).

Gene–environment interactions

None described. No GxE analyses exist for CCSK; the cohort sizes (largest molecular series 159 tumours) are far below what such analysis would require.

3. Phenotypes

CCSK's clinical phenotype is that of a large unilateral renal mass in a toddler. Frequencies below are drawn from the clinicopathological series cited; where a precise frequency is not published I say so rather than estimate one.
